========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it’s honestly been a really strange and overwhelming couple of weeks, and I’m just trying to make sense of it all, you know? What brings me in is that my body just feels completely out of my control. It’s like I’m disconnected from how I move. At first, maybe a few weeks ago, I had all this energy, just buzzing. I was pacing around my apartment all night—literally all night long—making these incredibly detailed notes for a supper club I wanted to start. I had menus planned out, seating charts, lighting concepts, the whole nine yards. I just couldn't sit still, my legs just had to keep moving, walking back and forth from the kitchen to the living room until the sun came up. But then, and this is the part that really scares me, it completely flipped. Lately, I’m just so, so slowed down. It feels like I’m moving through molasses. My sister actually had to come over because I wasn't answering my phone, and she got so frustrated with me because she said it takes me forever just to do the simplest things, like getting my shoes on. I sit on the edge of the bed with the shoe in my hand, and my brain says 'put the shoe on,' but my body just... doesn't do it, at least not for a long time. It’s just so heavy and exhausting, and I don't understand why I went from a hundred miles an hour to barely being able to move my own feet.

Oh, fast doesn't even begin to cover it. It was honestly like... well, you know when you're watching TV and someone is just holding down the channel button, just flipping and flipping? It was exactly like that, but inside my head. 

I’d be standing at my kitchen counter, right? And I’d start writing down a recipe for the supper club—I had this idea for a really complex duck confit with a sour cherry reduction, something really high-end since I serve that kind of stuff at the restaurant all the time and I know exactly how much people will pay for it. But before I could even finish listing the ingredients on my notepad, my mind would just completely jump. Suddenly I’m obsessing over the branding and the logo design, picturing this very sleek, minimalist font, maybe embossed on heavy navy cardstock. 

And then, before I can even sketch that out, bam, I’m onto the next thing, thinking about how to pitch the whole concept to angel investors. I was trying to remember this one business class I took back when I was still in college, trying to draft a full financial plan in my head, and then two seconds later I'm opening twenty different tabs on my laptop looking up commercial real estate to rent a warehouse space downtown. 

It was just jumping from recipes to logos to investors to renting a space, just bouncing around so incredibly quickly. And sleep? Sleep was completely out of the question. I couldn't sleep at all, not even for a minute, because every single time I had one idea, it immediately set off three more. It was like this massive chain reaction that just wouldn't stop, and I felt like I just had to stay awake to keep up with it all before I lost the ideas.

No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like, was I seeing things that weren't there or hearing voices or thinking the CIA was after me or something like that, right? I really believed I could pull it off if I just kept working on it. But no, no hallucinations or weird delusions or anything like that, if that's what you mean. But before I could even finish listing the ingredients on my notepad, my mind would just completely jump. Suddenly I’m obsessing over the branding and the logo design, picturing this very sleek, minimalist font, maybe embossed on heavy navy cardstock. \n\nAnd then, before I can even sketch that out, bam, I’m onto the next thing, thinking about how to pitch the whole concept to angel investors. I was trying to remember this one business class I took back when I was still in college, trying to draft a full financial plan in my head, and then two seconds later I'm opening twenty different tabs on my laptop looking up commercial real estate to rent a warehouse space downtown. \n\nIt was just jumping from recipes to logos to investors to renting a space, just bouncing around so incredibly quickly. And sleep? Sleep was completely out of the question. I couldn't sleep at all, not even for a minute, because every single time I had one idea, it immediately set off three more. It was like this massive chain reaction that just wouldn't stop, and I felt like I just had to stay awake to keep up with it all before I lost the ideas.\n\nDoctor: During that time when your thoughts were racing and you were full of energy, did you ever feel like your mind was playing tricks on you, or did you experience any thoughts that others might find unusual or disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like, was I seeing things that weren't there or hearing voices or thinking the CIA was after me or something like that, right? No, definitely not. Everything I was thinking about, all those ideas for the supper club, the menus, the investors, the real estate—it was all completely grounded in reality. I mean, I work in a restaurant, I’m a waiter, I see how the back of house operates, I see how the front of house operates, I know the profit margins on a good bottle of wine versus a well drink. So none of it was disconnected from reality at all. It was just... accelerated, you know? \n\nLike, it was all very real, very possible stuff. People start supper clubs all the time, especially in my neighborhood. I was just thinking about it so intensely and so quickly that it probably looked crazy from the outside, but inside my head, it all made perfect, logical sense. There were no tricks, no weird paranoia or anything. Just this overwhelming, intense clarity about what I wanted to do and how I was going to do it, even if I couldn't actually get my hands to write it down fast enough. I just... I really believed I could pull it off if I just kept working on it. But no, no hallucinations or weird delusions or anything like that, if that's what you mean. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it's... it's just really bad right now. Down doesn't even really cover it. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Like, usually, I have all these things that keep me steady, things I really look forward to. Cooking is a huge one for me, obviously, with the restaurant work and everything. I usually love coming home after a shift and just experimenting with flavors, maybe taking some really nice, stylized food photos for my Instagram. I’ve actually got a pretty decent following for that, and I usually spend hours getting the lighting just right, adjusting the garnishes, making sure the plating is perfect. 

But lately? I look at my kitchen and I just feel... nothing. I haven't cooked a real meal in days. I’ve just been eating whatever is easiest to grab, if I eat at all. And it’s not just the cooking. I usually have music playing constantly—jazz, indie rock, whatever fits my mood—but now the apartment is just dead silent because I can't even be bothered to open Spotify and pick a playlist. 

And I usually play pickup soccer with some guys from the neighborhood on Sunday mornings down at the park. It's always a good time, we go get coffee after, but the last two weeks I just texted them that I couldn't make it. I couldn't even explain why, I just... I didn't want to go. I didn't want to run, I didn't want to talk to anyone. Even this volunteering thing I do, helping out at the community garden on alternate Saturdays—which I normally really care about, I helped them set up their whole composting system last year—I just completely blew it off. It all just feels so flat. It’s the weirdest feeling, because logically, in my head, I know I used to care about these things. I know I love soccer, I know I love cooking. But when I try to actually do them, or even just think about doing them, I just can't make myself feel it. The feeling is just... gone. It's like I'm completely empty inside, just heavy and empty all at the same time.

I mean, I know what you’re asking—like, was I seeing things that weren't there or hearing voices or thinking the CIA was after me or something like that, right? No, definitely not. Everything I was thinking about, all those ideas for the supper club, the menus, the investors, the real estate—it was all completely grounded in reality. I mean, I work in a restaurant, I’m a waiter, I see how the back of house operates, I see how the front of house operates, I know the profit margins on a good bottle of wine versus a well drink. So none of it was disconnected from reality at all. It was just... accelerated, you know? \n\nLike, it was all very real, very possible stuff. People start supper clubs all the time, especially in my neighborhood. I was just thinking about it so intensely and so quickly that it probably looked crazy from the outside, but inside my head, it all made perfect, logical sense. There were no tricks, no weird paranoia or anything. Just this overwhelming, intense clarity about what I wanted to do and how I was going to do it, even if I couldn't actually get my hands to write it down fast enough. I just... I really believed I could pull it off if I just kept working on it. But no, no hallucinations or weird delusions or anything like that, if that's what you mean. Just the racing thoughts and the energy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you've been feeling lately, especially during this time when you've been moving so slowly—have you also been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's just really bad right now. Down doesn't even really cover it. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Like, usually, I have all these things that keep me steady, things I really look forward to. Cooking is a huge one for me, obviously, with the restaurant work and everything. I usually love coming home after a shift and just experimenting with flavors, maybe taking some really nice, stylized food photos for my Instagram. I’ve actually got a pretty decent following for that, and I usually spend hours getting the lighting just right, adjusting the garnishes, making sure the plating is perfect. \n\nBut lately? I look at my kitchen and I just feel... nothing. I haven't cooked a real meal in days. I’ve just been eating whatever is easiest to grab, if I eat at all. And it’s not just the cooking. I usually have music playing constantly—jazz, indie rock, whatever fits my mood—but now the apartment is just dead silent because I can't even be bothered to open Spotify and pick a playlist. \n\nAnd I usually play pickup soccer with some guys from the neighborhood on Sunday mornings down at the park. It's always a good time, we go get coffee after, but the last two weeks I just texted them that I couldn't make it. I couldn't even explain why, I just... I didn't want to go. I didn't want to run, I didn't want to talk to anyone. Even this volunteering thing I do, helping out at the community garden on alternate Saturdays—which I normally really care about, I helped them set up their whole composting system last year—I just completely blew it off. It all just feels so flat. It’s the weirdest feeling, because logically, in my head, I know I used to care about these things. I know I love soccer, I know I love cooking. But when I try to actually do them, or even just think about doing them, I just can't make myself feel it. The feeling is just... gone. It's like I'm completely empty inside, just heavy and empty all at the same time.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had an episode where your mood was extremely elevated or irritable, along with the increased energy and racing thoughts, to the point where it was clearly different from your usual self and caused problems in your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it was... well, the whole thing actually started right around the time I got fired from the restaurant. Which, you know, normally getting fired is a huge deal, right? You'd think I'd be devastated or worried about rent or whatever. But honestly, when the manager pulled me aside and told me they were letting me go—he said something about me being too erratic on the floor, which whatever, I was just working faster than everyone else—I didn't even care. Actually, it was the exact opposite. I walked out of there and I felt... I felt completely unstoppable. Like, weirdly, intensely excited. It was like a weight had been lifted and suddenly the universe was just opening up all these doors for me. I remember walking down the street and feeling like I was buzzing, like I was finally free to focus entirely on the supper club idea. I was on top of the world. I really was.

But... the thing is, it wasn't just that I was happy. It was like this aggressive kind of happy, if that makes sense? Because I had all this energy and all these massive plans, and I was so sure they were going to work out, but if anybody—and I mean anybody—even slightly questioned what I was doing, I would just absolutely lose it. 

Like, my roommate, Dave. I came home that day and I was pacing around our living room, just talking a mile a minute about the angel investors and the commercial real estate, and he just asked me, very calmly, he was like, "Hey man, shouldn't you maybe look for another serving job first just to cover rent this month?" And I just exploded. I mean, I went off on him. I started yelling about how he was small-minded, how he was trying to sabotage my future, how he just couldn't see the vision because he was stuck in his boring corporate job. I felt this intense, burning rage, like he was actively trying to ruin my life just by telling me to slow down. I couldn't handle anyone trying to put the brakes on me. It was like, if you weren't 100% on board with my brilliant plan right that second, you were the enemy. And looking back, it definitely caused problems... Dave barely spoke to me for weeks after that, and honestly, I don't blame him, because I was just so completely out of control with how angry I'd get if anyone tried to rein me in.

I mean, I work in a restaurant, I’m a waiter, I see how the back of house operates, I see how the front of house operates, I know the profit margins on a good bottle of wine versus a well drink. So none of it was disconnected from reality at all. It was just... accelerated, you know? \n\nLike, it was all very real, very possible stuff. People start supper clubs all the time, especially in my neighborhood. I was just thinking about it so intensely and so quickly that it probably looked crazy from the outside, but inside my head, it all made perfect, logical sense. There were no tricks, no weird paranoia or anything. Just this overwhelming, intense clarity about what I wanted to do and how I was going to do it, even if I couldn't actually get my hands to write it down fast enough. I just... I really believed I could pull it off if I just kept working on it. But no, no hallucinations or weird delusions or anything like that, if that's what you mean. Just the racing thoughts and the energy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you've been feeling lately, especially during this time when you've been moving so slowly—have you also been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's just really bad right now. Down doesn't even really cover it. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Like, usually, I have all these things that keep me steady, things I really look forward to. Cooking is a huge one for me, obviously, with the restaurant work and everything. I usually love coming home after a shift and just experimenting with flavors, maybe taking some really nice, stylized food photos for my Instagram. I’ve actually got a pretty decent following for that, and I usually spend hours getting the lighting just right, adjusting the garnishes, making sure the plating is perfect. \n\nBut lately? I look at my kitchen and I just feel... nothing. I haven't cooked a real meal in days. I’ve just been eating whatever is easiest to grab, if I eat at all. And it’s not just the cooking. I usually have music playing constantly—jazz, indie rock, whatever fits my mood—but now the apartment is just dead silent because I can't even be bothered to open Spotify and pick a playlist. \n\nAnd I usually play pickup soccer with some guys from the neighborhood on Sunday mornings down at the park. It's always a good time, we go get coffee after, but the last two weeks I just texted them that I couldn't make it. I couldn't even explain why, I just... I didn't want to go. I didn't want to run, I didn't want to talk to anyone. Even this volunteering thing I do, helping out at the community garden on alternate Saturdays—which I normally really care about, I helped them set up their whole composting system last year—I just completely blew it off. It all just feels so flat. It’s the weirdest feeling, because logically, in my head, I know I used to care about these things. I know I love soccer, I know I love cooking. But when I try to actually do them, or even just think about doing them, I just can't make myself feel it. The feeling is just... gone. It's like I'm completely empty inside, just heavy and empty all at the same time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where your mood was extremely elevated or irritable, along with the increased energy and racing thoughts, to the point where it was clearly different from your usual self and caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was... well, the whole thing actually started right around the time I got fired from the restaurant. Which, you know, normally getting fired is a huge deal, right? You'd think I'd be devastated or worried about rent or whatever. But honestly, when the manager pulled me aside and told me they were letting me go—he said something about me being too erratic on the floor, which whatever, I was just working faster than everyone else—I didn't even care. Actually, it was the exact opposite. I walked out of there and I felt... I felt completely unstoppable. Like, weirdly, intensely excited. It was like a weight had been lifted and suddenly the universe was just opening up all these doors for me. I remember walking down the street and feeling like I was buzzing, like I was finally free to focus entirely on the supper club idea. I was on top of the world. I really was.\n\nBut... the thing is, it wasn't just that I was happy. It was like this aggressive kind of happy, if that makes sense? Because I had all this energy and all these massive plans, and I was so sure they were going to work out, but if anybody—and I mean anybody—even slightly questioned what I was doing, I would just absolutely lose it. \n\nLike, my roommate, Dave. I came home that day and I was pacing around our living room, just talking a mile a minute about the angel investors and the commercial real estate, and he just asked me, very calmly, he was like, \"Hey man, shouldn't you maybe look for another serving job first just to cover rent this month?\" And I just exploded. I mean, I went off on him. I started yelling about how he was small-minded, how he was trying to sabotage my future, how he just couldn't see the vision because he was stuck in his boring corporate job. I felt this intense, burning rage, like he was actively trying to ruin my life just by telling me to slow down. I couldn't handle anyone trying to put the brakes on me. It was like, if you weren't 100% on board with my brilliant plan right that second, you were the enemy. And looking back, it definitely caused problems... Dave barely spoke to me for weeks after that, and honestly, I don't blame him, because I was just so completely out of control with how angry I'd get if anyone tried to rein me in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Oh, yeah. Yeah, it went way beyond just yelling at Dave or losing that one serving job. It was... well, it was a complete disaster, honestly. 

Because, okay, with the rent... usually Dave and I split it, right? I always Venmo him my half on the 28th of the month, and he pays the landlord on the 1st. We've done it that way for two years. But that month, I just... I didn't do it. I had some of the money—well, I had it before I started buying up all these expensive website domain names for the supper club—but I just completely ignored his texts about it. I remember looking at my phone and thinking, "Why is he bothering me about a thousand bucks when I'm about to secure half a million in seed funding?" So that whole rent arrangement just completely fell apart. We actually got a warning notice on our door, which is insane because I've never been late on rent in my life. 

And my other work plans? Forget it. I know I told you I got fired, but before that happened, I actually had trial shifts lined up at a couple of really high-end, Michelin-star places downtown. Places I had been networking for months to get into. And I just ghosted them. I didn't show up, I didn't call, I didn't email. I couldn't hold any of my work plans together because I was just too busy pacing around the city, entirely consumed by my own ideas. 

And then it spilled over into my family, too, which is what really brought everything crashing down. I completely dropped the ball on all my family commitments. My mom had this big 60th birthday dinner that my sister and I had been planning together for literally six months. We had a whole shared spreadsheet for the catering and the guests. And the day of the party, I just... I didn't go. I was in the middle of writing this massive, chaotic business plan on a bunch of legal pads and I just turned my phone off. 

My sister was furious, obviously, but then she got really scared. Because when she finally drove over to my apartment a couple days later to confront me, she saw how I was actually living. The place was a total wreck. I hadn't showered, there were papers and dirty coffee cups everywhere, I hadn't bought groceries in I don't even know how long. I couldn't get even the most basic responsibilities organized on my own anymore. It was like I forgot how to just be a functioning person because my brain was vibrating at this crazy, unbearable frequency. She took one look at me, told me to put my shoes on, and she actually had to drive me straight to the crisis clinic downtown. She literally walked me inside herself because I just... I couldn't hold my life together anymore. It had all just completely unraveled.

Can you tell me what’s been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s honestly been a really strange and overwhelming couple of weeks, and I’m just trying to make sense of it all, you know? What brings me in is that my body just feels completely out of my control. It’s like I’m disconnected from how I move. At first, maybe a few weeks ago, I had all this energy, just buzzing. I was pacing around my apartment all night—literally all night long—making these incredibly detailed notes for a supper club I wanted to start. I had menus planned out, seating charts, lighting concepts, the whole nine yards. I just couldn't sit still, my legs just had to keep moving, walking back and forth from the kitchen to the living room until the sun came up. But then, and this is the part that really scares me, it completely flipped. Lately, I’m just so, so slowed down. It feels like I’m moving through molasses. My sister actually had to come over because I wasn't answering my phone, and she got so frustrated with me because she said it takes me forever just to do the simplest things, like getting my shoes on. I sit on the edge of the bed with the shoe in my hand, and my brain says 'put the shoe on,' but my body just... doesn't do it, at least not for a long time. It’s just so heavy and exhausting, and I don't understand why I went from a hundred miles an hour to barely being able to move my own feet.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what was going through your mind during that time when you felt so energetic and couldn’t sit still? Were you feeling unusually confident, needing less sleep, or thinking faster than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, fast doesn't even begin to cover it. It was honestly like... well, you know when you're watching TV and someone is just holding down the channel button, just flipping and flipping? It was exactly like that, but inside my head. \n\nI’d be standing at my kitchen counter, right? And I’d start writing down a recipe for the supper club—I had this idea for a really complex duck confit with a sour cherry reduction, something really high-end since I serve that kind of stuff at the restaurant all the time and I know exactly how much people will pay for it. But before I could even finish listing the ingredients on my notepad, my mind would just completely jump. Suddenly I’m obsessing over the branding and the logo design, picturing this very sleek, minimalist font, maybe embossed on heavy navy cardstock. \n\nAnd then, before I can even sketch that out, bam, I’m onto the next thing, thinking about how to pitch the whole concept to angel investors. I was trying to remember this one business class I took back when I was still in college, trying to draft a full financial plan in my head, and then two seconds later I'm opening twenty different tabs on my laptop looking up commercial real estate to rent a warehouse space downtown. \n\nIt was just jumping from recipes to logos to investors to renting a space, just bouncing around so incredibly quickly. And sleep? Sleep was completely out of the question. I couldn't sleep at all, not even for a minute, because every single time I had one idea, it immediately set off three more. It was like this massive chain reaction that just wouldn't stop, and I felt like I just had to stay awake to keep up with it all before I lost the ideas.\n\nDoctor: During that time when your thoughts were racing and you were full of energy, did you ever feel like your mind was playing tricks on you, or did you experience any thoughts that others might find unusual or disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like, was I seeing things that weren't there or hearing voices or thinking the CIA was after me or something like that, right? No, definitely not. Everything I was thinking about, all those ideas for the supper club, the menus, the investors, the real estate—it was all completely grounded in reality. I mean, I work in a restaurant, I’m a waiter, I see how the back of house operates, I see how the front of house operates, I know the profit margins on a good bottle of wine versus a well drink. So none of it was disconnected from reality at all. It was just... accelerated, you know? \n\nLike, it was all very real, very possible stuff. People start supper clubs all the time, especially in my neighborhood. I was just thinking about it so intensely and so quickly that it probably looked crazy from the outside, but inside my head, it all made perfect, logical sense. There were no tricks, no weird paranoia or anything. Just this overwhelming, intense clarity about what I wanted to do and how I was going to do it, even if I couldn't actually get my hands to write it down fast enough. I just... I really believed I could pull it off if I just kept working on it. But no, no hallucinations or weird delusions or anything like that, if that's what you mean. Just the racing thoughts and the energy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you've been feeling lately, especially during this time when you've been moving so slowly—have you also been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's just really bad right now. Down doesn't even really cover it. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Like, usually, I have all these things that keep me steady, things I really look forward to. Cooking is a huge one for me, obviously, with the restaurant work and everything. I usually love coming home after a shift and just experimenting with flavors, maybe taking some really nice, stylized food photos for my Instagram. I’ve actually got a pretty decent following for that, and I usually spend hours getting the lighting just right, adjusting the garnishes, making sure the plating is perfect. \n\nBut lately? I look at my kitchen and I just feel... nothing. I haven't cooked a real meal in days. I’ve just been eating whatever is easiest to grab, if I eat at all. And it’s not just the cooking. I usually have music playing constantly—jazz, indie rock, whatever fits my mood—but now the apartment is just dead silent because I can't even be bothered to open Spotify and pick a playlist. \n\nAnd I usually play pickup soccer with some guys from the neighborhood on Sunday mornings down at the park. It's always a good time, we go get coffee after, but the last two weeks I just texted them that I couldn't make it. I couldn't even explain why, I just... I didn't want to go. I didn't want to run, I didn't want to talk to anyone. Even this volunteering thing I do, helping out at the community garden on alternate Saturdays—which I normally really care about, I helped them set up their whole composting system last year—I just completely blew it off. It all just feels so flat. It’s the weirdest feeling, because logically, in my head, I know I used to care about these things. I know I love soccer, I know I love cooking. But when I try to actually do them, or even just think about doing them, I just can't make myself feel it. The feeling is just... gone. It's like I'm completely empty inside, just heavy and empty all at the same time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where your mood was extremely elevated or irritable, along with the increased energy and racing thoughts, to the point where it was clearly different from your usual self and caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was... well, the whole thing actually started right around the time I got fired from the restaurant. Which, you know, normally getting fired is a huge deal, right? You'd think I'd be devastated or worried about rent or whatever. But honestly, when the manager pulled me aside and told me they were letting me go—he said something about me being too erratic on the floor, which whatever, I was just working faster than everyone else—I didn't even care. Actually, it was the exact opposite. I walked out of there and I felt... I felt completely unstoppable. Like, weirdly, intensely excited. It was like a weight had been lifted and suddenly the universe was just opening up all these doors for me. I remember walking down the street and feeling like I was buzzing, like I was finally free to focus entirely on the supper club idea. I was on top of the world. I really was.\n\nBut... the thing is, it wasn't just that I was happy. It was like this aggressive kind of happy, if that makes sense? Because I had all this energy and all these massive plans, and I was so sure they were going to work out, but if anybody—and I mean anybody—even slightly questioned what I was doing, I would just absolutely lose it. \n\nLike, my roommate, Dave. I came home that day and I was pacing around our living room, just talking a mile a minute about the angel investors and the commercial real estate, and he just asked me, very calmly, he was like, \"Hey man, shouldn't you maybe look for another serving job first just to cover rent this month?\" And I just exploded. I mean, I went off on him. I started yelling about how he was small-minded, how he was trying to sabotage my future, how he just couldn't see the vision because he was stuck in his boring corporate job. I felt this intense, burning rage, like he was actively trying to ruin my life just by telling me to slow down. I couldn't handle anyone trying to put the brakes on me. It was like, if you weren't 100% on board with my brilliant plan right that second, you were the enemy. And looking back, it definitely caused problems... Dave barely spoke to me for weeks after that, and honestly, I don't blame him, because I was just so completely out of control with how angry I'd get if anyone tried to rein me in.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if your elevated mood and increased energy ever led to significant problems at work, with friends, or in other areas of your life, beyond what you’ve already described?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, it went way beyond just yelling at Dave or losing that one serving job. It was... well, it was a complete disaster, honestly. \n\nBecause, okay, with the rent... usually Dave and I split it, right? I always Venmo him my half on the 28th of the month, and he pays the landlord on the 1st. We've done it that way for two years. But that month, I just... I didn't do it. I had some of the money—well, I had it before I started buying up all these expensive website domain names for the supper club—but I just completely ignored his texts about it. I remember looking at my phone and thinking, \"Why is he bothering me about a thousand bucks when I'm about to secure half a million in seed funding?\" So that whole rent arrangement just completely fell apart. We actually got a warning notice on our door, which is insane because I've never been late on rent in my life. \n\nAnd my other work plans? Forget it. I know I told you I got fired, but before that happened, I actually had trial shifts lined up at a couple of really high-end, Michelin-star places downtown. Places I had been networking for months to get into. And I just ghosted them. I didn't show up, I didn't call, I didn't email. I couldn't hold any of my work plans together because I was just too busy pacing around the city, entirely consumed by my own ideas. \n\nAnd then it spilled over into my family, too, which is what really brought everything crashing down. I completely dropped the ball on all my family commitments. My mom had this big 60th birthday dinner that my sister and I had been planning together for literally six months. We had a whole shared spreadsheet for the catering and the guests. And the day of the party, I just... I didn't go. I was in the middle of writing this massive, chaotic business plan on a bunch of legal pads and I just turned my phone off. \n\nMy sister was furious, obviously, but then she got really scared. Because when she finally drove over to my apartment a couple days later to confront me, she saw how I was actually living. The place was a total wreck. I hadn't showered, there were papers and dirty coffee cups everywhere, I hadn't bought groceries in I don't even know how long. I couldn't get even the most basic responsibilities organized on my own anymore. It was like I forgot how to just be a functioning person because my brain was vibrating at this crazy, unbearable frequency. She took one look at me, told me to put my shoes on, and she actually had to drive me straight to the crisis clinic downtown. She literally walked me inside herself because I just... I couldn't hold my life together anymore. It had all just completely unraveled.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I didn't want to run, I didn't want to talk to anyone. But honestly, when the manager pulled me aside and told me they were letting me go—he said something about me being too erratic on the floor, which whatever, I was just working faster than everyone else—I didn't even care. No... no, never. That's the crazy part about all of this, honestly. I've never... this is the very first time anything like this has ever happened to me. 

I mean, sure, I've had times in my life where I was in a really good mood, you know? Like when I first moved to the city and got my first real apartment, or when I got my first promotion at the restaurant. I was excited, maybe I stayed up a little late celebrating with friends, but it was just normal guy-in-his-twenties stuff. It wasn't... it wasn't *this*. 

I've always been a pretty level-headed person, honestly. Even back in high school, and during the couple of years I spent at community college before I decided to drop out and really focus on the hospitality industry, I was always the reliable one. Like, if there was a group project, I was the one organizing it. If my friends were going too hard at a party, I was the guy making sure everyone got an Uber home safe. I've always just been very steady. 

So to go from that—from being the guy everyone relies on to pay the rent on the exact day it's due, the guy who networks and shows up early for his shifts—to... to whatever this was, this absolute tornado that just tore through my entire life... it's just completely out of left field. My sister even said that to the intake nurse when she dropped me off at the clinic. I remember her standing there, crying, and she kept saying, "He's never been like this, he's never acted this way in his entire 25 years of life." 

And she's right, because I haven't. I've never felt that kind of unstoppable, buzzing, vibrating energy before, and I've definitely never gotten that aggressively angry at people just for asking me simple, logical questions like Dave did. It's like I was a completely different person for those few weeks. I don't even know where that guy came from because he's never been in my head before. So, yeah, no, this is the first time. Absolutely the first time anything like this has ever happened.

Yeah, it went way beyond just yelling at Dave or losing that one serving job. It was... well, it was a complete disaster, honestly. \n\nBecause, okay, with the rent... usually Dave and I split it, right? I always Venmo him my half on the 28th of the month, and he pays the landlord on the 1st. We've done it that way for two years. But that month, I just... I didn't do it. I had some of the money—well, I had it before I started buying up all these expensive website domain names for the supper club—but I just completely ignored his texts about it. I remember looking at my phone and thinking, \"Why is he bothering me about a thousand bucks when I'm about to secure half a million in seed funding?\" So that whole rent arrangement just completely fell apart. I couldn't hold any of my work plans together because I was just too busy pacing around the city, entirely consumed by my own ideas. \n\nAnd then it spilled over into my family, too, which is what really brought everything crashing down. I couldn't get even the most basic responsibilities organized on my own anymore. I couldn't hold my life together anymore. It had all just completely unraveled.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before—where your mood and energy were extremely elevated or irritable, causing major disruptions in your life—or has this been the first time you've gone through something like this?\n\nPatient: No... no, never. Yeah, actually... yeah. Before all this manic, crazy energy started with the supper club idea, I did have this really long stretch where I just felt completely empty. It was probably for like a month, maybe a month and a half? It was right around the time the weather started getting cold, and I just... I couldn't pull myself out of it.

I would look at my guitar, which I used to play every single night after my shifts to unwind, and it was just like a piece of wood. I felt absolutely nothing. 

Same with cooking. I love cooking, obviously, being in the hospitality industry. I usually spend my days off trying out new recipes or going to the farmers market to get fresh ingredients, taking photos of the plating for my Instagram. But during that time, I couldn't even bring myself to boil pasta. I just ordered takeout or ate cereal out of the box because the thought of actually preparing a meal felt like climbing Mount Everest. 

And the soccer thing, too. I play in this Sunday league with some guys I've known since high school, and I'm usually the one hyping everyone up in the group chat, making sure we have enough guys for the roster. But I just stopped responding. They were texting me like, "Hey man, you coming this week?" and I just stared at the screen. It was like I knew I *used* to care about playing, I knew I *used* to love running around on the field and grabbing a beer with them after, but I just couldn't make myself feel it anymore. The connection between my brain and the things that usually brought me joy was just completely severed. I felt so incredibly heavy and hopeless, like I was just dragging myself through every single day, waiting to go back to sleep. So, yeah, I definitely had that before... before the switch flipped and I went totally off the rails.

Four days? Oh, man, I wish it was just four days. No, it... it was way, way longer than that. This whole thing has been going on for about four months. Yeah, four whole months of just... non-stop, full-throttle, out-of-my-mind energy.

I felt like I was walking on air, like getting fired was actually this massive breakthrough, like the universe was telling me I was meant for bigger things, you know? And from that exact moment on, for four solid months, it was every single day. 

I'd wake up at, like, 3 AM, if I even went to sleep at all, and my brain would just be firing on all cylinders. I'd be drafting those supper club menus, looking at commercial real estate listings, emailing potential investors before the sun even came up. It wasn't just a weekend thing or a quick four-day phase. It was a third of a year where I was just entirely consumed by this... this buzzing. 

Like I said before, I'm usually the reliable, steady guy. I'm the guy who pays rent on time. But for those four months, starting the literal minute I handed over my apron and walked out of that restaurant, I was just a completely different person. The energy never dipped, it never took a day off, it just kept building and building and building every single day until my sister finally had to drag me into that clinic. So, yeah, to answer your question, way more than four days. Four months.

Yeah, exactly, like I was just saying about those four months, it was every single day. But to get into the actual mood part of it, what it actually felt like in my head... yeah, it was extremely elevated, and then incredibly irritable. 

I mean, getting fired from a restaurant job usually crushes a guy, right? You worry about rent, you worry about how you're gonna buy groceries. But me? After I got fired, I felt weirdly unstoppable. I was just so incredibly excited about everything. I felt like I was vibrating with this pure, golden energy, like I had suddenly unlocked the cheat codes to the universe and getting fired was just the universe clearing my schedule so I could build my empire. I was on top of the world, sketching out these massive, elaborate plans for the supper club, convinced I was going to be a millionaire by the end of the year. 

But the flip side of that—and this is where the irritable part comes in—was that I had zero patience for anyone who wasn't on my exact wavelength. If anyone, and I mean *anyone*, questioned my plans or told me I needed to slow down, I would just completely lose it. I'd blow up at them like they were trying to ruin my life. 

Like my roommate Dave. I mentioned him earlier, right? He just asked me, very calmly, how I was going to pay for this massive commercial-grade standing mixer I had ordered to the apartment. Which, looking back, is a totally logical question. But in that moment? In my head, he wasn't just asking a financial question. He was actively trying to sabotage my destiny. He was the enemy, trying to crush my dreams because he was jealous of my vision. I screamed at him for like twenty minutes. I was pacing around the kitchen, waving my arms, telling him he was a small-minded idiot who was trying to keep me trapped in mediocrity. I just got so aggressively, violently angry over nothing, just because he wasn't matching my level of excitement and told me to maybe hit the brakes. 

It was like, you were either with me on this rocket ship, or you were actively trying to shoot me down. There was no in-between. So yeah, it was this crazy mix of feeling like an absolute god, but also wanting to rip the head off anyone who dared to tell me I was moving too fast. And that went on for the entire four months.

      "Onset clearly different from usual non-depressed mood",
      "Manic episode resulted in marked impairment in social and occupational functioning",
      "No psychotic features confirmed"
    ]
  }
